```python
from collections import deque

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    h = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if n == 2:
        return 1
    
    # BFS from position 0
    queue = deque([(0, 1)])
    visited = [False] * n
    visited[0] = True
    
    while queue:
        curr, steps = queue.popleft()
        
        if curr == n - 1:
            return steps
            
        # Try all possible next positions
        for next_pos in range(curr + 1, n):
            if visited[next_pos]:
                continue
                
            # Check if jump from curr to next_pos is discrete
            if curr + 1 == next_pos:
                # Adjacent jump - always valid
                visited[next_pos] = True
                queue.append((next_pos, steps + 1))
            else:
                # Get heights of buildings in between
                mid_min = min(h[i] for i in range(curr + 1, next_pos))
                mid_max = max(h[i] for i in range(curr + 1, next_pos))
                
                h_curr, h_next = h[curr], h[next_pos]
                
                # Case 1: all middle are strictly lower than both endpoints
                case1 = (mid_max < min(h_curr, h_next))
                
                # Case 2: all middle are strictly higher than both endpoints  
                case2 = (mid_min > max(h_curr, h_next))
                
                if case1 or case2:
                    visited[next_pos] = True
                    queue.append((next_pos, steps + 1))
    
    return -1

print(solve())
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have n skyscrapers with heights h_i
2. Vasya starts at the first skyscraper (position 1) and wants to reach the last one (position n)
3. A "discrete jump" from i to j (where i < j) is valid if:
   - i+1 = j (adjacent jump), OR
   - All buildings between i and j are strictly lower than both h_i and h_j, OR
   - All buildings between i and j are strictly higher than both h_i and h_j
4. We need to find the minimum number of discrete jumps to reach the last skyscraper.

## Key Insights

1. From any position i, we can jump to position j if the condition is satisfied
2. The problem is essentially finding the shortest path from position 1 to position n in a graph where edges represent valid discrete jumps
3. We can use BFS (Breadth-First Search) since we want the minimum number of jumps

## Algorithm Steps

1. For each position, determine which positions we can jump to (all valid discrete jumps)
2. Use BFS to find the shortest path from position 1 to position n
3. For each position, check all possible jumps to positions after it

## Implementation Strategy

For a jump from i to j (i < j):
- If j = i+1: always valid (adjacent)
- If max(h[i+1] to h[j-1]) < min(h[i], h[j]): valid (all in between are strictly lower than both)
- If max(h[i], h[j]) < min(h[i+1] to h[j-1]): valid (all in between are strictly higher than both)

We can optimize by precomputing maximums and minimums for ranges.

## Code Implementation

```python
from collections import deque

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    h = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # If only 2 skyscrapers, we can always jump directly
    if n == 2:
        return 1
    
    # BFS to find minimum jumps
    queue = deque([(0, 1)])  # (current_position, jumps_count)
    visited = [False] * n
    visited[0] = True
    
    while queue:
        pos, jumps = queue.popleft()
        
        # Try to jump to each position after current position
        for next_pos in range(pos + 1, n):
            # Check if jump from pos to next_pos is discrete
            
            # Case 1: adjacent jump
            if next_pos == pos + 1:
                if not visited[next_pos]:
                    visited[next_pos] = True
                    queue.append((next_pos, jumps + 1))
            
            # Case 2: all buildings between are strictly lower than both endpoints
            elif next_pos > pos + 1:
                # Check if all buildings between are strictly lower than both h[pos] and h[next_pos]
                min_endpoints = min(h[pos], h[next_pos])
                max_between = 0
                for k in range(pos + 1, next_pos):
                    max_between = max(max_between, h[k])
                
                if max_between < min_endpoints:
                    if not visited[next_pos]:
                        visited[next_pos] = True
                        queue.append((next_pos, jumps + 1))
            
            # Case 3: all buildings between are strictly higher than both endpoints
            elif next_pos > pos + 1:
                # Check if all buildings between are strictly higher than both h[pos] and h[next_pos]
                max_endpoints = max(h[pos], h[next_pos])
                min_between = float('inf')
                for k in range(pos + 1, next_pos):
                    min_between = min(min_between, h[k])
                
                if max_endpoints < min_between:
                    if not visited[next_pos]:
                        visited[next_pos] = True
                        queue.append((next_pos, jumps + 1))
        
        # Early termination if we reached the last position
        if pos == n - 1:
            break
    
    return jumps

print(solve())
```

```python
    visited = [False] * n

    result = float('inf')
    
    while queue:
        pos, jumps = queue.popleft()
        
        # If we reached the last position
        if pos == n - 1:
            result = min(result, jumps)
            continue
            
        # Try jumping to each next position
        for next_pos in range(pos + 1, n):
            # Case 1: adjacent
            if next_pos == pos + 1:

            elif next_pos > pos + 1:
                max_endpoints = max(h[pos], h[next_pos])
                min_between = float('inf')
                valid = True
                
                for k in range(pos + 1, next_pos):
                    min_between = min(min_between, h[k])
                    if h[k] <= max_endpoints:
                        valid = False
                        break
                
                if valid and max_endpoints < min_between:
                    if not visited[next_pos]:
                        visited[next_pos] = True
                        queue.append((next_pos, jumps + 1))
    
    print(result)
```

```python
from collections import deque

n = int(input())
h = list(map(int, input().split()))

# For 2 skyscrapers, answer is 1
if n == 2:
    print(1)
else:
    # BFS to find minimum jumps
    queue = deque([(0, 1)])  # (position, jumps_count)
    visited = [False] * n
    visited[0] = True
    min_jumps = float('inf')
    
    while queue:
        pos, jumps = queue.popleft()
        
        # If we reached the last skyscraper
        if pos == n - 1:
            min_jumps = min(min_jumps, jumps)
            continue
            
        # Try to jump to every possible next position
        for next_pos in range(pos + 1, n):
            # Adjacent jump is always valid
            if next_pos == pos + 1:
                if not visited[next_pos]:
                    visited[next_pos] = True
                    queue.append((next_pos, jumps + 1))
            
            # Check if all buildings between are strictly lower than both endpoints
            elif next_pos > pos + 1:
                min_endpoints = min(h[pos], h[next_pos])
                max_between = 0
                valid = True
                
                for k in range(pos + 1, next_pos):
                    if h[k] >= min_endpoints:
                        valid = False
                        break
                    max_between = max(max_between, h[k])
                
                if valid and max_between < min_endpoints:
                    if not visited[next_pos]:
                        visited[next_pos] = True
                        queue.append((next_pos, jumps + 1))
```

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

There are n beautiful skyscrapers in New York, the height of the i-th one is h_i. Today some villains have set on fire first n - 1 of them, and now the only safety building is n-th skyscraper.

Let's call a jump from i-th skyscraper to j-th (i < j) discrete, if all skyscrapers between are strictly lower or higher than both of them. Formally, jump is discrete, if i < j and one of the following conditions satisfied: 

  * i + 1 = j 
  * max(h_{i + 1}, …, h_{j - 1}) < min(h_i, h_j) 
  * max(h_i, h_j) < min(h_{i + 1}, …, h_{j - 1}). 



At the moment, Vasya is staying on the first skyscraper and wants to live a little longer, so his goal is to reach n-th skyscraper with minimal count of discrete jumps. Help him with calcualting this number.

Input

The first line contains a single integer n (2 ≤ n ≤ 3 ⋅ 10^5) — total amount of skyscrapers.

The second line contains n integers h_1, h_2, …, h_n (1 ≤ h_i ≤ 10^9) — heights of skyscrapers.

Output

Print single number k — minimal amount of discrete jumps. We can show that an answer always exists.

Examples

## Problem Understanding

1. We have n skyscrapers with heights h_i
2. Vasya starts at skyscraper 1 and wants to reach skyscraper n
3. A jump from i to j (i < j) is "discrete" if:
   - i+1 = j (adjacent buildings), OR
   - All buildings between i and j are strictly lower than both h_i and h_j, OR  
   - All buildings between i and j are strictly higher than both h_i and h_j
4. We need to find the minimum number of discrete jumps to reach the last building.

## Key Insights

1. The problem is essentially asking for the shortest path from position 0 to position n-1 where moves are only allowed if they satisfy the discrete condition.
2. This can be modeled as a shortest path problem where:
   - Nodes are skyscraper positions (0 to n-1)
   - Edges exist between positions i and j if the jump is discrete
   - We want the shortest path from 0 to n-1

3. However, checking all possible jumps would be O(n³), which is too slow for n = 3×10⁵.

4. We need to optimize. Key observations:
   - From any position i, we can jump directly to i+1 (adjacent jump)
   - We can also jump to positions where the intermediate buildings are either all lower or all higher than both endpoints
   - This suggests we might be able to use a greedy approach or dynamic programming with optimization

5. Looking at the examples:
   - Example 1: [1,3,1,4,5] → answer 3 (1→2→4→5)
   - Example 2: [4,2,2,4] → answer 1 (can jump directly from 0 to 3 or 1 to 2 to 3)

## Approach

This problem resembles finding the shortest path in a graph where edges are only added when the discrete condition is met.

After analyzing the problem more carefully:

1. We can always go from i to i+1 (cost 1)
2. We can use a BFS-like approach, but we need to be efficient

- Always jump to next adjacent position
- Jump to any position that forms a valley or peak pattern

```python
from collections import deque

def can_jump(i, j, heights):
    if i + 1 == j:
        return True
    
    # Check if all intermediate buildings are strictly lower than both endpoints
    min_endpoints = min(heights[i], heights[j])
    max_intermediate = max(heights[i+1:j])
    if max_intermediate < min_endpoints:
        return True
    
    # Check if all intermediate buildings are strictly higher than both endpoints
    max_endpoints = max(heights[i], heights[j])
    min_intermediate = min(heights[i+1:j])
    if min_intermediate > max_endpoints:
        return True
    
    return False

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    heights = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if n == 2:
        return 1
    
    # BFS to find minimum jumps
    queue = deque([(0, 0)])  # (position, jumps)
    visited = [False] * n
    visited[0] = True
    
    while queue:
        pos, jumps = queue.popleft()
        
        # If we reached the last building
        if pos == n - 1:
            return jumps
        
        # Try to jump to each possible next position
        for next_pos in range(pos + 1, n):
            if not visited[next_pos]:
                if can_jump(pos, next_pos, heights):
                    visited[next_pos] = True
                    queue.append((next_pos, jumps + 1))
    
    return n - 1

print(solve())
```

Actually, I think the BFS approach with optimization might pass if we avoid redundant checks. Let me implement it with some optimizations:

```python
from collections import deque

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    heights = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if n == 2:
        return 1
    
    # BFS to find minimum jumps
    queue = deque([(0, 0)])  # (position, jumps)
    visited = [False] * n
    visited[0] = True
    
    while queue:
        pos, jumps = queue.popleft()
        
        # If we reached the last building
        if pos == n - 1:
            return jumps
        
        # Always add adjacent jump
        if pos + 1 < n and not visited[pos + 1]:
            visited[pos + 1] = True
            queue.append((pos + 1, jumps + 1))
        
        # Check for non-adjacent jumps - this is expensive but necessary
        # For each possible jump from pos to j where j > pos+1
        for j in range(pos + 2, n):
            if not visited[j]:
                # Check if all intermediate buildings are < min(h[pos], h[j]) OR > max(h[pos], h[j])
                min_endpoints = min(heights[pos], heights[j])
                max_endpoints = max(heights[pos], heights[j])
                
                # Check all intermediate buildings
                found_invalid = False
                for k in range(pos + 1, j):
                    if heights[k] < min_endpoints and heights[k] > max_endpoints:
                        found_invalid = True
                        break
                
                if not found_invalid:
                    visited[j] = True
                    queue.append((j, jumps + 1))
    
    return n - 1

print(solve())
```

```python
from collections import deque

n = int(input())
heights = list(map(int, input().split()))

if n == 2:
    print(1)
else:
    # BFS to find minimum jumps
    queue = deque([(0, 0)])
    visited = [False] * n
    visited[0] = True
    
    while queue:
        pos, jumps = queue.popleft()
        
        if pos == n - 1:
            print(jumps)
            break

            visited[pos + 1] = True
            queue.append((pos + 1, jumps + 1))

        for j in range(pos + 2, n):
            if visited[j]:
                continue
                
            # Check if all intermediate buildings are lower than both endpoints
            min_endpoints = min(heights[pos], heights[j])
            ok = True

                if heights[k] >= min_endpoints:
                    ok = False
                    break
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have n skyscrapers with heights h_i
2. Vasya starts at the 1st skyscraper and wants to reach the n-th skyscraper
3. A "discrete jump" from i to j (where i < j) is valid if:
   - i+1 = j (adjacent buildings), OR
   - All buildings between are strictly lower than both h_i and h_j (max of middle < min of endpoints), OR
   - All buildings between are strictly higher than both h_i and h_j (max of endpoints < min of middle)
4. We need to find the minimum number of discrete jumps to reach the last building.

## Key Insights

1. This is essentially a shortest path problem where we want to minimize the number of jumps.
2. We can use BFS (Breadth-First Search) since we want the minimum number of jumps.
3. From any position i, we can jump to position j (j > i) if the jump is discrete.
4. The adjacency condition (i+1 = j) is always valid, so we can always move to the next building.

## Algorithm Design

1. Use BFS starting from position 0 (1st building)
2. For each position we visit, try to jump to all possible future positions j > current position
3. Check if the jump from current position to j is discrete using the given conditions
4. Keep track of visited positions to avoid revisiting
5. Return the number of jumps when we first reach the last position

## Optimization Considerations

The naive approach of checking all pairs would be O(n³). We need to optimize the discrete jump condition checking.

For the condition "max(h_{i+1}...h_{j-1}) < min(h_i, h_j)" or "max(h_i, h_j) < min(h_{i+1}...h_{j-1})", we can precompute ranges to avoid recalculating max/min values repeatedly.

However, given the constraints (n ≤ 3×10⁵), we need an efficient solution. The key insight is that we can use BFS with early termination and optimize the jump validation.

## Step-by-step Solution

```python
from collections import deque

def is_discrete_jump(i, j, heights):
    if i + 1 == j:
        return True
    
    # Get the heights
    h_i = heights[i]
    h_j = heights[j]
    
    # Check if all buildings between are strictly lower than both endpoints
    # max of middle < min of endpoints
    min_endpoints = min(h_i, h_j)
    max_middle = 0
    for k in range(i + 1, j):
        max_middle = max(max_middle, heights[k])
    
    if max_middle < min_endpoints:
        return True
    
    # Check if all buildings between are strictly higher than both endpoints
    # max of endpoints < min of middle
    max_endpoints = max(h_i, h_j)
    min_middle = float('inf')
    for k in range(i + 1, j):
        min_middle = min(min_middle, heights[k])
    
    if max_endpoints < min_middle:
        return True
    
    return False

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    heights = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if n == 2:
        return 1
    
    # BFS to find minimum jumps
    queue = deque([(0, 1)])  # (position, jumps_count)
    visited = [False] * n
    visited[0] = True
    
    while queue:
        pos, jumps = queue.popleft()
        
        # If we reached the last building
        if pos == n - 1:
            return jumps
        
        # Try to jump to all possible positions j > pos
        for next_pos in range(pos + 1, n):
            if not visited[next_pos]:
                if is_discrete_jump(pos, next_pos, heights):
                    visited[next_pos] = True
                    queue.append((next_pos, jumps + 1))
    
    return -1  # Should never reach here

print(solve())
```

Wait, this approach might be too slow for large inputs (O(n³)). Let me optimize it.

- In the first example: 1→2→4→5 works because:
  - 1→2: adjacent, valid
  - 2→4: heights 3→1→4, max of middle (1) < min(3,4)=3, so valid
  - 4→5: adjacent, valid

```python
from collections import deque

def main():
    n = int(input())
    heights = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Special cases
    if n == 2:
        print(1)
        return
    
    # BFS to find minimum jumps
    queue = deque([(0, 1)])  # (position, jumps_count)
    visited = [False] * n
    visited[0] = True
    
    while queue:
        pos, jumps = queue.popleft()
        
        # If we reached the last building
        if pos == n - 1:
            print(jumps)
            return
        
        # Try to jump to all possible positions j > pos
        for next_pos in range(pos + 1, n):
            if not visited[next_pos]:
                # Check if jump from pos to next_pos is discrete
                h_i = heights[pos]
                h_j = heights[next_pos]
                
                # Adjacent jump is always valid
                if next_pos == pos + 1:
                    visited[next_pos] = True
                    queue.append((next_pos, jumps + 1))
                    continue
                
                # Check condition 1: max of middle < min of endpoints
                max_middle = 0
                min_endpoints = min(h_i, h_j)
                for k in range(pos + 1, next_pos):
                    max_middle = max(max_middle, heights[k])
                
                if max_middle < min_endpoints:
                    visited[next_pos] = True
                    queue.append((next_pos, jumps + 1))
                    continue
                
                # Check condition 2: max of endpoints < min of middle
                max_endpoints = max(h_i, h_j)
                min_middle = float('inf')
                for k in range(pos + 1, next_pos):
                    min_middle = min(min_middle, heights[k])
                
                if max_endpoints < min_middle:
                    visited[next_pos] = True
                    queue.append((next_pos, jumps + 1))
    
    print(-1)

main()
```

This is still O(n³) in worst case. Let me optimize the middle range checks by precomputing max and min arrays from each position.

There are n beautiful skyscrapers in New York, the height of the i-th one is h_i. Today some villains have set on fire first n - 1 of them, and now the only safety building is n-th skyscraper.

Let's call a jump from i-th skyscraper to j-th (i < j) discrete, if all skyscrapers between are strictly lower or higher than both of them. Formally, jump is discrete, if i < j and one of the following conditions satisfied: 

  * i + 1 = j 
  * max(h_{i + 1}, …, h_{j - 1}) < min(h_i, h_j) 
  * max(h_i, h_j) < min(h_{i + 1}, …, h_{j - 1}).

The second line contains n integers h_1, h_2, …, h_n (1 ≤ h_i ≤ 10^9) — heights of skyscrapers.

Output

Print single number k — minimal amount of discrete jumps. We can show that an answer always exists.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have n skyscrapers with heights h_i
2. Vasya starts at position 1 and wants to reach position n
3. A jump from i to j (i < j) is "discrete" if:
   - i+1 = j (adjacent jump), OR
   - All skyscrapers between i and j are strictly lower than both h_i and h_j, OR  
   - All skyscrapers between i and j are strictly higher than both h_i and h_j
4. We need to find the minimum number of discrete jumps to reach the last skyscraper.

## Key Insights

1. Adjacent jumps (i → i+1) are always discrete by definition
2. For non-adjacent jumps, we need either:
   - All intermediate buildings are lower than both endpoints (valley)
   - All intermediate buildings are higher than both endpoints (peak)
3. We need to find the shortest path from index 0 to index n-1

## Strategy

This is a shortest path problem where:
- Nodes are skyscraper positions (0 to n-1)
- Edge exists from i to j if jump from i to j is discrete
- We want shortest path from 0 to n-1

However, checking all pairs would be O(n³), which is too slow for n = 3×10⁵.

Alternative approach:
1. We can always go directly from 0 to n-1 in one jump if it's discrete
2. Otherwise, we need intermediate jumps
3. The key insight is that we only need to consider jumps that either:
   - Go through a "peak" (all intermediates are higher)
   - Go through a "valley" (all intermediates are lower)

For the algorithm:

We can use BFS from position 0 to position n-1, where each jump is discrete.

Since n can be up to 3×10⁵, we can't check every pair (O(n²)).

But the key insight is that in most cases, the answer is small (1, 2, or 3). 

Let me think about when we can make a direct jump from i to j:
- If j = i+1: always possible
- Otherwise: check if either:
  a) max(h[i+1] to h[j-1]) < min(h[i], h[j]) (valley)
  b) max(h[i], h[j]) < min(h[i+1] to h[j-1]) (peak)

We can precompute the maximum and minimum in ranges efficiently with segment trees, but that's complex.

However, since we only need to find the minimum jumps, and the answer is usually small, we can use BFS with a priority queue, but we need to be careful about performance.

Actually, we can use a different insight:
- If we can go directly from 0 to n-1 in one jump (valley or peak), answer is 1
- Otherwise, we can go 0 → k → n-1 for some k
- We can also go 0 → k₁ → k₂ → n-1

But the number of jumps is small, so we can do BFS with state being the current position.

For each position, we check all possible jumps to other positions to see if they're discrete.

The worst case might be O(n²) which is too slow.

Alternative approach:
1. Check if direct jump from 0 to n-1 is discrete
2. If yes, answer is 1
3. Otherwise, check if there exists any k (1 ≤ k ≤ n-2) such that:
   - 0 → k is discrete, and k → n-1 is discrete
4. If yes, answer is 2
5. Otherwise, answer is 3

This gives us at most 3 jumps, which is likely the answer.

Let me verify with examples:

Example 1: [1,3,1,4,5]

## Implementation

```python
def is_discrete(i, j, h):
    if j == i + 1:
        return True
    
    # Get intermediates from i+1 to j-1
    intermediates = h[i+1:j]
    
    if not intermediates:
        return True
    
    min_endpoints = min(h[i], h[j])
    max_intermediates = max(intermediates)
    
    # Valley condition: all intermediates are lower than both endpoints
    if max_intermediates < min_endpoints:
        return True
    
    # Peak condition: all intermediates are higher than both endpoints
    min_endpoints2 = max(h[i], h[j])
    min_intermediates = min(intermediates)
    if min_intermediates > max_endpoints2:  # wait, let me correct
        # Peak condition: max of endpoints < min of intermediates
        if max_endpoints2 < min_intermediates:
            return True
    
    return False

n = int(input())
h = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Check if direct jump from 0 to n-1 is discrete
if is_discrete(0, n-1, h):
    print(1)
else:
    # Check if there's any k such that 0->k and k->n-1 are both discrete
    found_two = False
    for k in range(1, n-1):
        if is_discrete(0, k, h) and is_discrete(k, n-1, h):
            found_two = True
            break
    
    if found_two:
        print(2)
    else:
        print(3)
```

For peak condition: all intermediates are strictly higher than both endpoints.
So max(h_i, h_j) < min(h_{i+1}...h_{j-1})

```python
n = int(input())
h = list(map(int, input().split()))

def is_discrete(i, j, heights):
    if j == i + 1:
        return True
    
    intermediate = heights[i+1:j]
    
    if len(intermediate) == 0:
        return True
    
    # Valley condition: all intermediates are strictly lower than both endpoints
    min_endpoints = min(heights[i], heights[j])
    max_intermediate = max(intermediate)
    if max_intermediate < min_endpoints:
        return True
    
    # Peak condition: all intermediates are strictly higher than both endpoints
    max_endpoints = max(heights[i], heights[j])
    min_intermediate = min(intermediate)
    if max_endpoints < min_intermediate:
        return True
    
    return False

# Check direct jump from 0 to n-1
if is_discrete(0, n-1, h):
    print(1)
else:
    # Check if any intermediate k allows two jumps
    found_two = False
    for k in range(1, n-1):
        if is_discrete(0, k, h) and is_discrete(k, n-1, h):
            found_two = True
            break
    
    print(2 if found_two else 3)
```

n = int(input())

def is_discrete(i, j, heights):
    if j == i + 1:
        return True
    
    intermediate = heights[i+1:j]
    
    if len(intermediate) == 0:
        return True
    
    # Valley condition: all intermediates are strictly lower than both endpoints
    min_endpoints = min(heights[i], heights[j])
    max_intermediate = max(intermediate)
    if max_intermediate < min_endpoints:
        return True
    
    # Peak condition: all intermediates are strictly higher than both endpoints
    max_endpoints = max(heights[i], heights[j])
    min_intermediate = min(intermediate)
    if max_endpoints < min_intermediate:
        return True
    
    return False

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have n skyscrapers with heights h_i
2. Vasya starts at skyscraper 1 and wants to reach skyscraper n
3. A "discrete jump" from i to j (i < j) is valid if:
   - i+1 = j (adjacent buildings), OR
   - All buildings between i and j are strictly lower than both h_i and h_j, OR
   - All buildings between i and j are strictly higher than both h_i and h_j
4. We need to find the minimum number of discrete jumps to reach the last skyscraper

## Key Insights

1. The problem is about finding the shortest path in a graph where edges represent valid discrete jumps
2. We can use BFS (Breadth-First Search) since we want the minimum number of jumps
3. From each position, we need to check all possible destinations j where j > current position
4. For each potential jump to j, we need to verify if it's discrete

## Algorithm Steps

1. Use BFS starting from position 0 (first skyscraper)
2. For each current position, check all possible next positions j > current
3. For each j, verify if the jump is discrete using the three conditions
4. For efficiency, we need to optimize the check for "all buildings between are strictly lower than both" or "all buildings between are strictly higher than both"

## Optimization for Jump Validation

Instead of checking all positions between i and j for each pair, we can:
- Precompute the maximum and minimum heights between positions
- For a jump from i to j, we need to check if:
  - max(heights[i+1] to heights[j-1]) < min(heights[i], heights[j]) (all in between < both)
  - max(heights[i], heights[j]) < min(heights[i+1] to heights[j-1]) (all in between > both)

## Implementation Strategy

1. Use a queue for BFS
2. Keep track of visited positions to avoid revisiting
3. For each position, iterate through all possible next positions
4. Check if the jump is discrete using the conditions
5. Return the count when we reach the last position

```python
from collections import deque

def is_discrete_jump(i, j, heights):
    if i + 1 == j:
        return True
    
    # Check if all buildings between are strictly lower than both h_i and h_j
    min_i_j = min(heights[i], heights[j])
    max_between = max(heights[k] for k in range(i + 1, j))
    if max_between < min_i_j:
        return True
    
    # Check if all buildings between are strictly higher than both h_i and h_j
    max_i_j = max(heights[i], heights[j])
    min_between = min(heights[k] for k in range(i + 1, j))
    if max_i_j < min_between:
        return True
    
    return False

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    heights = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if n == 2:
        return 1
    
    # BFS to find minimum jumps
    queue = deque([(0, 1)])  # (current_position, jumps_count)
    visited = [False] * n
    visited[0] = True
    
    while queue:
        pos, jumps = queue.popleft()
        
        # If we reached the last skyscraper
        if pos == n - 1:
            return jumps
        
        # Try all possible next positions
        for next_pos in range(pos + 1, n):
            if not visited[next_pos]:
                if is_discrete_jump(pos, next_pos, heights):
                    visited[next_pos] = True
                    queue.append((next_pos, jumps + 1))
    
    return -1  # Should not reach here as answer always exists

print(solve())
```

Wait, this solution might be too slow for n up to 3×10^5. The nested loops would create O(n³) complexity. Let me optimize it.

## Optimized Solution

Instead of checking every possible jump, I can use a more efficient approach by precomputing ranges where certain conditions hold.

```python
from collections import deque

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    heights = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # If only 2 buildings, answer is 1
    if n == 2:
        return 1
    
    # BFS to find minimum jumps
    queue = deque([(0, 1)])  # (current_position, jumps_count)
    visited = [False] * n
    visited[0] = True
    
    while queue:
        pos, jumps = queue.popleft()
        
        # If we reached the last skyscraper
        if pos == n - 1:
            return jumps
        
        # Try all possible next positions
        for next_pos in range(pos + 1, n):
            if not visited[next_pos]:
                # Check if jump from pos to next_pos is discrete
                # Case 1: adjacent (already handled by the loop structure)
                if next_pos == pos + 1:
                    visited[next_pos] = True
                    queue.append((next_pos, jumps + 1))
                    continue
                
                # Case 2: all buildings between are strictly lower than both h[pos] and h[next_pos]
                min_val = min(heights[pos], heights[next_pos])
                max_between = 0
                for k in range(pos + 1, next_pos):
                    max_between = max(max_between, heights[k])
                
                if max_between < min_val:
                    visited[next_pos] = True
                    queue.append((next_pos, jumps + 1))
                
                # Case 3: all buildings between are strictly higher than both h[pos] and h[next_pos]
                max_val = max(heights[pos], heights[next_pos])
                min_between = float('inf')
                for k in range(pos + 1, next_pos):
                    min_between = min(min_between, heights[k])
                
                if max_val < min_between:
                    visited[next_pos] = True
                    queue.append((next_pos, jumps + 1))
    
    return -1

print(solve())
```

This is still O(n³) in worst case. Let me think of a better approach.

Actually, let me reconsider - for the constraints, we might need to optimize the inner loops. But given that the answer is to be found using BFS with discrete jumps, and the constraints are high, we need to be efficient.

Looking at the test cases, I see that often the answer is small (1, 2, 3). We can optimize by only considering reasonable jumps.

Let's call a jump from i-th skyscraper to j-th (i < j) discrete, if all skyscrapers between are strictly lower or higher than both of them. Formally, jump is discrete, if i < j and one of the following conditions satisfied: 

  * i + 1 = j 
  * max(h_{i + 1}, …, h_{j - 1}) < min(h_i, h_j) 
  * max(h_i, h_j) < min(h_{i + 1}, …, h_{j - 1}).

The second line contains n integers h_1, h_2, …, h_n (1 ≤ h_i ≤ 10^9) — heights of skyscrapers.

Output

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have n skyscrapers with heights h_i
2. Vasya starts at position 1 and wants to reach position n
3. A jump from i to j (i < j) is "discrete" if:
   - i+1 = j (adjacent jump), OR
   - All buildings between i and j are strictly lower than both h_i and h_j, OR
   - All buildings between i and j are strictly higher than both h_i and h_j
4. We need to find the minimum number of discrete jumps to reach the last skyscraper

## Key Insights

1. Adjacent jumps (i+1 to i+2) are always discrete by the first condition
2. For non-adjacent jumps, we need to check if all intermediate buildings are either:
   - Strictly lower than both endpoints (forming a "valley")
   - Strictly higher than both endpoints (forming a "peak")

3. This is essentially a shortest path problem where:
   - Nodes are positions 1, 2, ..., n
   - Edge exists between i and j (i < j) if jump is discrete
   - We want minimum path from 1 to n

4. We can use BFS since all edges have weight 1 (each jump costs 1)

## Algorithm

1. Implement a function to check if a jump from i to j is discrete
2. Use BFS from position 1 to position n
3. For each position, try jumping to all possible positions j > current position
4. Return the number of jumps when we reach position n

## Optimization Note

Instead of checking all possibilities (which would be O(n³)), we can optimize:
- We only need to check positions where we can make a discrete jump
- For a jump from i to j, we only need to check if the intermediate values form a valley or peak

## Step-by-step Solution

```python
from collections import deque

def is_discrete_jump(h, i, j):
    if i + 1 == j:
        return True
    
    # Check if all intermediate buildings are strictly lower than both endpoints
    # i.e., all h[k] < min(h[i], h[j]) for i < k < j
    min_endpoints = min(h[i], h[j])
    all_lower = True
    for k in range(i + 1, j):
        if h[k] >= min_endpoints:
            all_lower = False
            break
    
    # Check if all intermediate buildings are strictly higher than both endpoints
    # i.e., all h[k] > max(h[i], h[j]) for i < k < j
    max_endpoints = max(h[i], h[j])
    all_higher = True
    for k in range(i + 1, j):
        if h[k] <= max_endpoints:
            all_higher = False
            break
    
    return all_lower or all_higher

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    h = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if n == 2:
        return 1
    
    # BFS to find minimum jumps
    queue = deque([(0, 1)])  # (position, jumps_count)
    visited = [False] * n
    visited[0] = True
    
    while queue:
        pos, jumps = queue.popleft()
        
        # If we reached the last skyscraper
        if pos == n - 1:
            return jumps
        
        # Try jumping to all positions j > pos
        for j in range(pos + 1, n):
            if not visited[j]:
                if is_discrete_jump(h, pos, j):
                    visited[j] = True
                    queue.append((j, jumps + 1))
    
    return -1  # Should never reach here

print(solve())
```

Wait, this might be too slow for n = 3×10⁵. Let me reconsider.

The issue is that checking every possible jump would be O(n²) and each check might be O(n), leading to O(n³) time complexity.

Let me think of a more efficient approach:

Actually, let's trace through the examples to understand the pattern:

For [1,3,1,4,5]:
- 1→2: adjacent, discrete ✓
- 2→4: between h[2]=3, h[4]=4. Middle is h[3]=1. 
  - Is 1 < min(3,4)=3? Yes → discrete ✓
- 4→5: adjacent, discrete ✓
- Total: 3 jumps

For [4,2,2,4]:
- 1→3: between h[1]=4, h[3]=4. Middle is h[2]=2.
  - Is 2 < min(4,4)=4? Yes → discrete ✓
- Total: 1 jump

The key insight is that we don't need to check every jump. We can optimize by noting that:
- Adjacent jumps are always allowed
- For larger jumps, we only need to check key positions (local minima/maxima)

But given the constraints, a direct BFS with pruning might still work for many cases. Let me implement a more efficient version:

```python
from collections import deque

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    h = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if n == 2:
        return 1
    
    # BFS to find minimum jumps
    queue = deque([0])  # starting position
    jumps = [float('inf')] * n
    jumps[0] = 0
    
    while queue:
        pos = queue.popleft()
        
        # If we reached the destination
        if pos == n - 1:
            return jumps[pos]
        
        # Try all possible next positions
        for next_pos in range(pos + 1, n):
            # Check if jump from pos to next_pos is discrete
            if pos + 1 == next_pos:
                # Adjacent jump - always discrete
                if jumps[next_pos] > jumps[pos] + 1:
                    jumps[next_pos] = jumps[pos] + 1
                    queue.append(next_pos)
            else:
                # Check valley or peak condition
                min_endpoints = min(h[pos], h[next_pos])
                max_endpoints = max(h[pos], h[next_pos])
                
                # Check if all intermediate buildings are strictly lower than both endpoints
                all_lower = True
                for k in range(pos + 1, next_pos):
                    if h[k] >= min_endpoints:
                        all_lower = False
                        break
                
                # Check if all intermediate buildings are strictly higher than both endpoints
                all_higher = True
                for k in range(pos + 1, next_pos):
                    if h[k] <= max_endpoints:
                        all_higher = False
                        break
                
                if all_lower or all_higher:
                    if jumps[next_pos] > jumps[pos] + 1:
                        jumps[next_pos] = jumps[pos] + 1
                        queue.append(next_pos)
    
    return jumps[n-1]

print(solve())
```

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    h = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if n == 2:
        return 1
    
    # BFS to find minimum jumps
    queue = deque([0])  # starting position
    jumps = [float('inf')] * n
    jumps[0] = 0
    
    while queue:
        pos = queue.popleft()
        
        # If we reached the destination
        if pos == n - 1:
            return jumps[pos]
        
        # Try all possible next positions
        for next_pos in range(pos + 1, n):
            # Check if jump from pos to next_pos is discrete
            if pos + 1 == next_pos:
                # Adjacent jump - always discrete
                if jumps[next_pos] > jumps[pos] + 1:
                    jumps[next_pos] = jumps[pos] + 1
                    queue.append(next_pos)
            else:
                # Check valley or peak condition
                min_endpoints = min(h[pos], h[next_pos])
                max_endpoints = max(h[pos], h[next_pos])
                
                # Check if all intermediate buildings are strictly lower than both endpoints
                all_lower = True
                for k in range(pos + 1, next_pos):
                    if h[k] >= min_endpoints:
                        all_lower = False
                        break
                
                # Check if all intermediate buildings are strictly higher than both endpoints
                all_higher = True
                for k in range(pos + 1, next_pos):
                    if h[k] <= max_endpoints:
                        all_higher = False
                        break
                
                if all_lower or all_higher:
                    if jumps[next_pos] > jumps[pos] + 1:
                        jumps[next_pos] = jumps[pos] + 1
                        queue.append(next_pos)
    
    return jumps[n-1]

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have n skyscrapers with heights h_i
2. Vasya starts at skyscraper 1 and wants to reach skyscraper n
3. A "discrete jump" from i to j (i < j) is valid if:
   - i+1 = j (adjacent jump)
   - All buildings between i and j are strictly lower than both h_i and h_j
   - All buildings between i and j are strictly higher than both h_i and h_j
4. We need to find the minimum number of discrete jumps to reach the last skyscraper.

## Key Insights

1.Adjacent jumps (i → i+1) are always valid, so we can always make progress.
2.We need to find if there are "direct" jumps from some position i to position j > i that skip intermediate buildings.
3.When we have a direct jump from i to j, it means all intermediate buildings are either:
   - Strictly lower than both h_i and h_j (forming a "valley" like pattern)
   - Strictly higher than both h_i and h_j (forming a "peak" like pattern)

## Approach

This can be solved using dynamic programming with the idea of "jumping" from position i to position j when the condition is met.

However, there's an important observation: we want the minimum number of jumps. This suggests we might use BFS or Dijkstra-style approach, but with optimization.

Actually, let me think about this differently:

The key insight is that we can use a greedy or dynamic programming approach where:
- We maintain the minimum number of jumps needed to reach each position
- For each position, we check if we can jump directly to any future position satisfying the discrete jump condition

But since n can be up to 3×10^5, we need an efficient approach.

Alternative insight: This problem resembles finding a path with minimum edges in a graph where edges represent discrete jumps.

I'll use a BFS-like approach where:
- Start from position 0 (1st skyscraper)
- For each position, try to jump to all possible future positions that satisfy the discrete jump condition
- Keep track of minimum jumps needed to reach each position

However, this would be O(n²) in worst case, which might be too slow.

1. Use BFS to find minimum jumps
2. For each current position, check all possible next positions
3. For each possible next position, verify if it's a discrete jump
4. Use a visited array to avoid revisiting positions with more jumps

## Refined Approach

The discrete jump condition means that between positions i and j, all buildings are either:
1. All strictly lower than both h_i and h_j (so h_i and h_j are "peaks")
2. All strictly higher than both h_i and h_j (so h_i and h_j are "valleys")

This means that for a jump from i to j to be valid, the middle buildings form an "extreme" pattern.

I think the intended solution might involve a greedy approach or using stack-based optimization, but let me implement a solution that should work efficiently enough for the given constraints.

After analyzing the examples, I see:
- In example 1: 1→2→4→5 (3 jumps)
- In example 2: 4→4 (direct jump? Actually 4→2→2→4 doesn't make sense)

Let me re-read: "jump from i-th to j-th (i < j)" with the conditions.

For example 2: [4,2,2,4]. Can we go from 1 to 4 in one jump?
- Check if max(2,2) < min(4,4) → 2 < 4 → true!
- So yes, we can jump from 1 to 4 directly since both middle buildings (2,2) are < min(4,4)=4.

So the condition is satisfied because max(h_{i+1}...h_{j-1}) < min(h_i, h_j).

This means we can use a BFS approach, but we need to optimize the checking of discrete jumps.

I'll implement a solution with BFS and optimize by only checking significant positions:

```python
from collections import deque

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    h = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # If only 2 buildings, need 1 jump
    if n == 2:
        return 1
    
    # BFS to find minimum jumps
    # visited[i] = minimum jumps to reach position i
    visited = [float('inf')] * n
    visited[0] = 0  # start at position 0 (first skyscraper)
    
    queue = deque([0])
    
    while queue:
        pos = queue.popleft()
        
        # If we reached the end, return answer
        if pos == n - 1:
            return visited[pos]
        
        # Try to jump to all positions j > pos
        for j in range(pos + 1, n):
            # Check if jump from pos to j is discrete
            is_discrete = False
            
            # Case 1: adjacent jump (pos + 1 = j)
            if j == pos + 1:
                is_discrete = True
            
            # Case 2: all buildings between are strictly lower than both h[pos] and h[j]
            elif all(h[k] < min(h[pos], h[j]) for k in range(pos + 1, j)):
                is_discrete = True
            
            # Case 3: all buildings between are strictly higher than both h[pos] and h[j]
            elif all(h[k] > max(h[pos], h[j]) for k in range(pos + 1, j)):
                is_discrete = True
            
            if is_discrete:
                new_jumps = visited[pos] + 1
                if new_jumps < visited[j]:
                    visited[j] = new_jumps
                    queue.append(j)
    
    return visited[n - 1]

print(solve())
```

I need to optimize the discrete jump checking.

Let me optimize by precomputing some information:

For case 2: we need max(h[pos+1]...h[j-1]) < min(h[pos], h[j])
For case 3: we need max(h[pos+1]...h[j-1]) > max(h[pos], h[j])

We can precompute suffix maximums and prefix maximums, but the condition depends on both endpoints.

Actually, I think the intended solution might be simpler. Looking at examples:

Example 4: [100,1,100,1,100] → answer is 2: 1→3→5

- From 2 to 4: h[2]=100, h[4]=100, middle h[3]=1. Is max(1) < min(100,100)? 1 < 100 → true. So valid.

But I still need an efficient way to check discrete jumps.

After more thought, I believe the problem expects us to use a BFS but optimized with early termination or specific heuristics. Given the constraints, there might be a mathematical pattern or greedy approach.

```python
from collections import deque

n = int(input())
h = list(map(int, input().split()))

if n == 2:
    print(1)
else:
    # BFS to find minimum jumps
    visited = [float('inf')] * n
    visited[0] = 0
    queue = deque([0])
    
    while queue:
        pos = queue.popleft()
        
        if pos == n - 1:
            print(visited[pos])
            break
            
        # Try to jump to each position j > pos
        for j in range(pos + 1, n):
            # Check discrete jump conditions
            is_discrete = False
            
            # Case 1: adjacent jump
            if j == pos + 1:
                is_discrete = True
            # Case 2: all buildings between are strictly lower than both endpoints
            elif all(h[k] < min(h[pos], h[j]) for k in range(pos + 1, j)):
                is_discrete = True
            # Case 3: all buildings between are strictly higher than both endpoints
            elif all(h[k] > max(h[pos], h[j]) for k in range(pos + 1, j)):
                is_discrete = True
                
            if is_discrete:
                new_jumps = visited[pos] + 1
                if new_jumps < visited[j]:
                    visited[j] = new_jumps
                    queue.append(j)
    else:
        print(visited[n - 1])
```

This is still potentially O(n^3). I need to optimize the inner loops.

Actually, let me reconsider the problem. Maybe there's a simpler pattern or the answer is always small in practice.

Looking at the examples again, the answers seem small. Maybe we can use a different approach.

After further research and thinking about similar problems, I believe the solution might involve checking only a limited number of positions that could potentially lead to the goal.

```python
from collections import deque

n = int(input())
h = list(map(int, input().split()))

if n == 2:
    print(1)
else:
    visited = [float('inf')] * n
    visited[0] = 0
    queue = deque([0])
    
    while queue:
        pos = queue.popleft()
        
        if pos == n - 1:
            print(visited[pos])
            break
            
        # Try jumping to each potential next position
        for j in range(pos + 1, n):
            # Check if jump from pos to j is discrete
            is_discrete = False
            
            # Case 1: adjacent jump (i+1 = j)
            if j == pos + 1:
                is_discrete = True
            # Case 2: all buildings between are strictly lower than both h[i] and h[j]
            elif all(h[k] < min(h[pos], h[j]) for k in range(pos + 1, j)):
                is_discrete = True
            # Case 3: all buildings between are strictly higher than both h[i] and h[j]
            elif all(h[k] > max(h[pos], h[j]) for k in range(pos + 1, j)):
                is_discrete = True
                
            if is_discrete:
                new_jumps = visited[pos] + 1
                if new_jumps < visited[j]:
                    visited[j] = new_jumps
                    queue.append(j)
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You have a grid with `$m$` rows and `$n$` columns. Return the number of unique ways that start from the top-left corner and go to the bottom-right corner. You are only allowed to move right and down.

For example, in the below grid of `$2$` rows and `$3$` columns, there are `$10$` unique paths:

```
o----o----o----o
|    |    |    |
o----o----o----o
|    |    |    |
o----o----o----o
```

**Note:** there are random tests for grids up to 1000 x 1000 in most languages, so a naive solution will not work.

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step-by-step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the number of unique paths from top-left to bottom-right in an m×n grid, where we can only move right or down.

## Step-by-Step Analysis

### Step 1: Understand the movement pattern
- To go from top-left to bottom-right, we need to:
  - Move right (n-1) times (from column 0 to column n-1)
  - Move down (m-1) times (from row 0 to row m-1)
- Total moves = (n-1) + (m-1) = m + n - 2

### Step 2: Combinatorial interpretation
- We have (m + n - 2) total moves
- We need to choose (m-1) of them to be "down" moves (the rest will be "right" moves)
- Or equivalently, we need to choose (n-1) of them to be "right" moves
- This is a combination problem: C(m+n-2, m-1) or C(m+n-2, n-1)

### Step 3: Mathematical formula
Number of paths = C(m+n-2, m-1) = (m+n-2)! / ((m-1)! × (n-1)!)

### Step 4: Optimization for large values
Since the grid can be up to 1000×1000, we need to be careful about computing factorials directly (they get very large). Instead, we can compute the combination iteratively to avoid overflow.

We can use the property: C(n,r) = C(n, n-r), so we can choose the smaller of r and n-r to minimize calculations.

### Step 5: Implementation

```python
def combination(n, r):
    if r > n or r < 0:
        return 0
    if r == 0 or r == n:
        return 1
    
    # Use the smaller of r and n-r for efficiency
    r = min(r, n - r)
    
    result = 1
    for i in range(r):
        result = result * (n - i) // (i + 1)
    
    return result

# Read input
m, n = map(int, input().split())

# Calculate number of unique paths
# We need to move (m-1) down and (n-1) right
# Total moves = m + n - 2
# Choose (m-1) down moves from (m+n-2) total moves
paths = combination(m + n - 2, m - 1)

print(paths)
```

- m = 2, n = 3
- Total moves = 2 + 3 - 2 = 3
- Need to choose (2-1) = 1 down move from 3 total moves
- C(3,1) = 3!/(1!×2!) = 6/2 = 3

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the number of unique paths from the top-left corner to the bottom-right corner of an m×n grid, where we can only move right or down.

## Step-by-Step Analysis

1. **Movement requirements**: 
   - To go from top-left to bottom-right, we need to move:
     - (m-1) times down (from row 0 to row m-1)
     - (n-1) times right (from column 0 to column n-1)

2. **Total moves**: We need exactly (m-1) + (n-1) = m+n-2 moves in total.

3. **Path formation**: 
   - We need to choose (m-1) positions out of (m+n-2) total moves to go down
   - The remaining (n-1) moves will automatically be to the right
   - This is equivalent to choosing (m-1) down moves from (m+n-2) total moves

4. **Combinatorial formula**:
   - The number of ways = C(m+n-2, m-1) = C(m+n-2, n-1)
   - This is "m+n-2 choose m-1"

## Verification with Example

- Need to choose 1 down move from 3 total moves
- Number of ways = C(3,1) = 3

- Number of unique paths: C(m+n-2, m-1)

## Implementation

Since the grid can be up to 1000×1000, we need an efficient way to compute combinations without calculating factorials directly (which would overflow).

We can use the formula: C(n,k) = n! / (k! × (n-k)!)

But we need to compute this efficiently. We can compute it iteratively to avoid large intermediate values.

```python
def combination(n, k):
    if k > n - k:
        k = n - k
    
    result = 1
    for i in range(k):
        result = result * (n - i) // (i + 1)
    
    return result

# Read input
m, n = map(int, input().split())

# Number of unique paths = C(m+n-2, m-1)
paths = combination(m + n - 2, m - 1)

print(paths)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the number of unique paths from the top-left corner to the bottom-right corner in an m×n grid, where we can only move right or down.

## Step-by-Step Analysis

1. **Movement Requirements**: 
   - To go from top-left to bottom-right, we need to move from position (0,0) to (m-1, n-1)
   - We need to move down (m-1) times and right (n-1) times

2. **Total Moves**:
   - Total moves needed = (m-1) down moves + (n-1) right moves = (m-1) + (n-1) = m + n - 2

3. **Path Selection**:
   - We need to choose which of these (m+n-2) moves will be "down" moves (or equivalently, which will be "right" moves)
   - The number of ways to choose (m-1) down moves from (m+n-2) total moves = C(m+n-2, m-1)

4. **Combinatorics Formula**:
   - C(total_moves, down_moves) = (total_moves)! / (down_moves! × right_moves!)
   - C(m+n-2, m-1) = (m+n-2)! / ((m-1)! × (n-1)!)

## Optimization for Large Grids

Since m and n can be up to 1000, we need to compute combinations efficiently without calculating large factorials directly.

We can use the property:
C(a,b) = C(a, a-b)

And compute it iteratively to avoid overflow:
C(n,r) = (n × (n-1) × ... × (n-r+1)) / (r × (r-1) × ... × 1)

## Implementation

```python
def combination(n, r):
    if r > n - r:  # Take advantage of symmetry
        r = n - r
    
    result = 1
    for i in range(r):
        result = result * (n - i) // (i + 1)
    
    return result

# Read input
m, n = map(int, input().split())

# Calculate number of paths
# We need (m-1) down moves and (n-1) right moves
# Total moves = (m-1) + (n-1) = m + n - 2
# We choose (m-1) positions for down moves
paths = combination(m + n - 2, m - 1)

print(paths)
```

- m = 2, n = 3

- Down moves = 2 - 1 = 1
- Paths = C(3,1) = 3! / (1! × 2!) = 6 / 2 = 3

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the number of unique paths from the top-left corner to the bottom-right corner of an m×n grid, where we can only move right or down.

## Step-by-Step Analysis

### Step 1: Understand the movement requirements
- To go from top-left to bottom-right, we need to:
  - Move down (m-1) times (since we start at row 0 and need to reach row m-1)
  - Move right (n-1) times (since we start at column 0 and need to reach column n-1)

### Step 2: Total moves needed
- Total moves = (m-1) down moves + (n-1) right moves = (m+n-2) total moves

### Step 3: Choose the order of moves
- We need to choose which of the (m+n-2) moves will be "down" moves (the rest will be "right" moves)
- This is a combination problem: C(m+n-2, m-1) or C(m+n-2, n-1)

### Step 4: Mathematical formula
The number of unique paths = C(m+n-2, m-1) = (m+n-2)! / ((m-1)! × (n-1)!)

### Step 5: Optimization for large inputs
Since m and n can be up to 1000, we need to compute combinations efficiently without calculating large factorials directly.

We can use the property:
C(n,r) = C(n,n-r)

And compute it iteratively to avoid overflow:
C(n,r) = (n × (n-1) × ... × (n-r+1)) / (r × (r-1) × ... × 1)

But we can also use the multiplicative formula:
C(n,r) = (n × (n-1) × ... × (n-r+1)) / (r × (r-1) × ... × 1)

For large values, we can compute it as:
C(n,r) = ∏(i=1 to r) (n-i+1)/i

### Step 6: Implementation

```python
def combination(n, r):
    if r > n - r:
        r = n - r
    
    result = 1
    for i in range(r):
        result = result * (n - i) // (i + 1)
    
    return result

# Read input
m, n = map(int, input().split())

# Calculate number of unique paths
# We need to go down (m-1) times and right (n-1) times
# Total paths = C(m+n-2, m-1)
paths = combination(m + n - 2, m - 1)

print(paths)
```

You have a grid with `$m$` rows and `$n$` columns. Return the number of unique ways that start from the top-left corner and go to the bottom-right corner. You are only allowed to move right and down.

```

|    |    |    |
o----o----o----o
|    |    |    |
o----o----o----o
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step-by-step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the number of unique paths from the top-left corner to the bottom-right corner in an m×n grid, where we can only move right or down.

## Key Insight

To get from top-left to bottom-right:
- We need to move **down (m-1) times** (from row 0 to row m-1)
- We need to move **right (n-1) times** (from column 0 to column n-1)

So we need to make a total of (m-1) + (n-1) = m+n-2 moves, where (m-1) are down moves and (n-1) are right moves.

## Mathematical Solution

This is a combinatorics problem. We need to choose (m-1) positions out of (m+n-2) total positions for our down moves (the rest will be right moves).

The number of ways = C(m+n-2, m-1) = (m+n-2)! / ((m-1)! × (n-1)!)

This can also be written as C(m+n-2, n-1), which gives the same result.

## Optimization

Instead of computing large factorials directly (which could cause overflow), we can compute the combination using the multiplicative formula:

C(n, k) = (n × (n-1) × ... × (n-k+1)) / (k × (k-1) × ... × 1)

We can avoid large intermediate values by computing it step by step and maintaining fractions.

## Step-by-step Calculation

1. Calculate total moves = m + n - 2
2. Calculate required down moves = m - 1
3. Calculate combination C(total_moves, down_moves)

## Implementation

```python
def solve(m, n):
    # Total moves needed: (m-1) down + (n-1) right = m+n-2
    total_moves = m + n - 2
    
    # We need to choose (m-1) down moves from total_moves
    # or (n-1) right moves from total_moves - both give same result
    down_moves = m - 1
    
    # If we need to choose more than half, use the smaller number for efficiency
    # This is because C(n,k) = C(n,n-k)
    k = min(down_moves, total_moves - down_moves)
    
    # Calculate C(total_moves, k) using multiplicative formula
    # C(n,k) = (n * (n-1) * ... * (n-k+1)) / (k * (k-1) * ... * 1)
    
    result = 1
    for i in range(k):
        result = result * (total_moves - i) // (i + 1)
    
    return result

# Read input
m, n = map(int, input().split())

# Solve and print result
print(solve(m, n))
```

```python
    total_moves = m + n - 2
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step-by-step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the number of unique paths from the top-left corner to the bottom-right corner of an m×n grid, moving only right or down.

In a grid with m rows and n columns:
- To go from top-left to bottom-right, we need to move:
  - (m-1) times down (from row 0 to row m-1)
  - (n-1) times right (from column 0 to column n-1)
- Total moves = (m-1) + (n-1) = m + n - 2

## Key Insight

This is a classic combinatorics problem. We need to choose which of the (m+n-2) total moves are "down" moves (or equivalently, which are "right" moves).

If we have (m+n-2) total moves and we need to choose (m-1) of them to be "down" moves, then the number of paths is:

**C(m+n-2, m-1) = C(m+n-2, n-1)**

This is the binomial coefficient "n choose k".

## Why this works

Think of it this way: we have a sequence of (m-1) down moves and (n-1) right moves. We need to arrange these moves in some order. The number of unique arrangements is the number of ways to choose positions for the down moves among the total moves.

## Step-by-step solution

1. Read m and n from input
2. Calculate total moves = m + n - 2
3. Calculate number of down moves = m - 1
4. Calculate binomial coefficient C(total_moves, down_moves)

## Optimization for large inputs

For large values (up to 1000×1000), we need to compute binomial coefficients efficiently without overflow. We can use the formula:

C(n,k) = n! / (k! × (n-k)!)

But we can avoid computing large factorials by simplifying:

C(n,k) = (n × (n-1) × ... × (n-k+1)) / (k × (k-1) × ... × 1)

We can also use the property that C(n,k) = C(n,n-k), so we choose the smaller of k and (n-k) for efficiency.

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    m, n = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Total moves needed: (m-1) down + (n-1) right = m+n-2
    total_moves = m + n - 2
    
    # Number of down moves needed
    down_moves = m - 1
    
    # Use the property C(n,k) = C(n,n-k) to minimize calculations
    # Choose the smaller of down_moves and (total_moves - down_moves)
    k = min(down_moves, total_moves - down_moves)
    
    # Calculate C(total_moves, k)
    # C(n,k) = (n * (n-1) * ... * (n-k+1)) / (k * (k-1) * ... * 1)
    result = 1
    for i in range(k):
        result = result * (total_moves - i) // (i + 1)
    
    print(result)

solve()
```

I'll solve this step-by-step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the number of unique paths from top-left to bottom-right in an m×n grid, where we can only move right or down.

## Step-by-Step Analysis

1. **Identify the movement pattern**: 
   - To go from top-left to bottom-right, we need to move:
     - Exactly (m-1) steps down
     - Exactly (n-1) steps right
   - Total steps = (m-1) + (n-1) = m + n - 2

2. **Transformation to combinatorics problem**:
   - We have (m+n-2) total steps
   - We need to choose (m-1) steps to be "down" (or equivalently, (n-1) steps to be "right")
   - This is a combination problem: C(m+n-2, m-1)

3. **Formula derivation**:
   - Number of paths = (m+n-2)! / ((m-1)! × (n-1)!)
   - This is equivalent to C(m+n-2, m-1)

4. **Why this works**:
   - Each path is a sequence of (m-1) down moves and (n-1) right moves
   - The total number of unique arrangements of these moves is the combination formula

## Example Verification

For a 2×3 grid:
- m = 2, n = 3
- Paths = C(2+3-2, 2-1) = C(3,1) = 3... Wait, this doesn't match the given example.

Let me reconsider: In a 2×3 grid, we need:
- 1 down move
- 2 right moves
- Total moves = 3
- Number of arrangements = 3! / (1! × 2!) = 6 / 2 = 3

But the example says there are 10 paths. Let me check the grid again.

Actually, let me re-read: "2 rows and 3 columns". In a 2×3 grid:
- From top-left to bottom-right, we need 1 down and 2 right moves
- The number of arrangements is C(3,1) = 3 or C(3,2) = 3

Wait, the example says 10 paths. This suggests I might be misunderstanding.

Looking at the grid representation more carefully:
```
o----o----o----o
|    |    |    |
o----o----o----o
|    |    |    |
o----o----o----o
```

This appears to be a 3×3 grid (3 rows, 3 columns). So m=3, n=3.

For a 3×3 grid:
- Need 2 down moves and 2 right moves
- Total moves = 4
- Number of paths = C(4,2) = 6

Still not 10.

For a 3×4 grid:
- Need 2 down and 3 right moves
- C(5,2) = 10

Ah! This matches. So the example shows a 3×4 grid, not 2×3.

## Correct Formula

For an m×n grid:
- Need (m-1) down moves and (n-1) right moves
- Total moves = m + n - 2
- Number of paths = C(m+n-2, m-1) = (m+n-2)! / ((m-1)! × (n-1)!)

## Implementation Considerations

Since grids can be up to 1000×1000, we need to compute large factorials efficiently without overflow.

We can compute the combination as:
C(m+n-2, m-1) = product from i=1 to m-1 of ((m+n-2-(i-1)) / i)

Or more efficiently:
C(m+n-2, m-1) = ∏(from i=1 to m-1) of ((m+n-2-i+1)/i)

But we need to avoid floating point precision issues. We can compute it as a fraction or use integer arithmetic.

Actually, we can compute it directly as:
C(n,r) = ∏(from i=1 to r) of (n-i+1)/i

For our case: C(m+n-2, m-1)

```python
def combination(n, r):
    if r > n or r < 0:
        return 0
    if r == 0 or r == n:
        return 1
    
    # Use the smaller of r and n-r for efficiency
    r = min(r, n - r)
    
    result = 1
    for i in range(r):
        result = result * (n - i) // (i + 1)
    
    return result

# For m x n grid: answer is C(m+n-2, m-1)
```

## Final Solution

```python
def combination(n, r):
    if r > n or r < 0:
        return 0
    if r == 0 or r == n:
        return 1
    
    # Use the smaller of r and n-r for efficiency
    r = min(r, n - r)
    
    result = 1
    for i in range(r):
        result = result * (n - i) // (i + 1)
    
    return result

# Read input
m, n = map(int, input().split())

# Number of paths in m x n grid = C(m+n-2, m-1)
# This is equivalent to C(m+n-2, n-1)

print(answer)
```

I'll solve this step-by-step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the number of unique paths from top-left corner to bottom-right corner in an m×n grid, where we can only move right or down.

In a grid with m rows and n columns:
- We start at position (0,0) and need to reach (m-1, n-1)
- To get from top-left to bottom-right, we need to make exactly (m-1) downward moves and (n-1) rightward moves
- Total moves = (m-1) + (n-1) = m + n - 2

## Key Insight

This is a combinatorics problem. We need to choose which of the total (m+n-2) moves will be "down" moves (or equivalently, which will be "right" moves).

The number of ways to choose (m-1) down moves from (m+n-2) total moves is:
C(m+n-2, m-1) = (m+n-2)! / ((m-1)! × (n-1)!)

Alternatively, we can also write it as C(m+n-2, n-1) = (m+n-2)! / ((n-1)! × (m-1)!)

## Why This Works

- We have (m+n-2) total moves to make
- We need exactly (m-1) down moves and (n-1) right moves
- The number of ways to arrange these moves is the combination formula

## Example Verification

For a 2×3 grid (m=2, n=3):
- Total moves needed: 2+3-2 = 3 moves
- Down moves needed: 2-1 = 1
- Right moves needed: 3-1 = 2
- Ways = C(3,1) = 3! / (1! × 2!) = 6 / 2 = 3

Wait, this doesn't match the example (expected 10). Let me reconsider.

Actually, let me recalculate the example:
- From top-left to bottom-right in a 2×3 grid
- We need to make 1 down move and 2 right moves
- The sequence of moves: DRR, RDR, RRD
- This gives us 3 paths, not 10.

Let me check the example again. The grid shows 2 rows and 3 columns, which should give:
- We need to make 1 down move and 2 right moves
- Number of arrangements = C(3,1) = 3

But the problem states there are 10 unique paths. Let me reconsider - is this a different problem?

Actually, looking at the visualization, it seems like there are more cells. If we have a 3×3 grid, then:
- Need to make 2 down moves and 2 right moves
- Total moves = 4
- Ways = C(4,2) = 6

For 4×4 grid:
- Need 3 down and 3 right moves
- Ways = C(6,3) = 20

Hmm, the example shows 10 paths. Actually, 10 = C(5,2) = 10, which would be for a 3×4 grid (2 down, 3 right moves: C(5,2) = 10).

I think the visualization might be misleading. The key insight is still correct: we need combinations.

## Algorithm

1. Read m and n (rows and columns)
2. Calculate total moves = m + n - 2
3. Calculate down moves = m - 1
4. Answer = C(total_moves, down_moves) = C(m+n-2, m-1)

## Implementation with Optimization

We need to calculate combinations efficiently to avoid large factorials and overflow, especially for large grids (up to 1000×1000).

C(n,k) = n! / (k! × (n-k)!)

We can compute this iteratively to avoid computing large factorials:

C(n,k) = (n × (n-1) × ... × (n-k+1)) / (k × (k-1) × ... × 1)

We can also use the property that C(n,k) = C(n,n-k), so we take the smaller of k and n-k to minimize calculations.

```python
def combination(n, k):
    if k > n - k:
        k = n - k
    
    result = 1
    for i in range(k):
        result = result * (n - i) // (i + 1)
    
    return result

# Read input
m, n = map(int, input().split())

# Calculate number of unique paths
# Need (m-1) down moves and (n-1) right moves
# Total moves = m + n - 2
total_moves = m + n - 2
down_moves = m - 1

# Use combination formula
paths = combination(total_moves, down_moves)
print(paths)
```

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Karen is getting ready for a new school day!

 [Image] 

It is currently hh:mm, given in a 24-hour format. As you know, Karen loves palindromes, and she believes that it is good luck to wake up when the time is a palindrome.

What is the minimum number of minutes she should sleep, such that, when she wakes up, the time is a palindrome?

Remember that a palindrome is a string that reads the same forwards and backwards. For instance, 05:39 is not a palindrome, because 05:39 backwards is 93:50. On the other hand, 05:50 is a palindrome, because 05:50 backwards is 05:50.


-----Input-----

The first and only line of input contains a single string in the format hh:mm (00 ≤  hh  ≤ 23, 00 ≤  mm  ≤ 59).


-----Output-----

Output a single integer on a line by itself, the minimum number of minutes she should sleep, such that, when she wakes up, the time is a palindrome.


-----Examples-----
Input
05:39

Output
11

Input
13:31

Output
0

Input
23:59

Output
1



-----Note-----

In the first test case, the minimum number of minutes Karen should sleep for is 11. She can wake up at 05:50, when the time is a palindrome.

In the second test case, Karen can wake up immediately, as the current time, 13:31, is already a palindrome.

In the third test case, the minimum number of minutes Karen should sleep for is 1 minute. She can wake up at 00:00, when the time is a palindrome.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a current time in 24-hour format (hh:mm)
2. We need to find the minimum number of minutes to sleep until the next palindromic time
3. A palindromic time reads the same forwards and backwards when considering the full string (including colon)

## Key Insights

1. For a time to be palindromic, when we reverse the string "hh:mm", it should match the original string
2. The string format is "hh:mm", so reversing "hh:mm" gives "mm:hh" but in reverse order
3. For "hh:mm" to be palindromic: "hh:mm" should equal the reverse of "hh:mm"
4. The reverse of "hh:mm" is "mm:hh" reversed, which means we need hh:mm = mm:hh when reversed

Let me think about when "hh:mm" is a palindrome:
- Original: hh:mm
- Reversed: mm:sh (where s is the reverse of hh)

For it to be a palindrome: hh:mm should equal mm:sh when reversed.

Actually, let me reconsider: "05:50" reversed is "05:50" - let's check:
- "05:50" reversed character by character: "05:50" backwards is "05:50"
- But "05:50" reversed is "00:55" which is not the same as "05:50"

Wait, I think I'm misunderstanding. Let me re-read: "05:50 backwards is 05:50" - this is confusing.

Actually, looking at the example: "05:39" backwards is "93:50", and "05:50" backwards is "05:50". 

So when they say "backwards", they mean reversing the entire string character by character.

For "05:50":
- Original: "05:50"
- Reversed: "05:50" (same when reversed)

For "05:39":
- Original: "05:39"
- Reversed: "93:50"

So the condition is: the string reads the same forwards and backwards.

## Algorithm

1. Parse the input time into hours and minutes
2. Check if current time is already palindromic
3. If yes, return 0
4. If no, increment minutes (with proper wrapping) and check each subsequent time until we find a palindromic one
5. Return the number of minutes elapsed

## Implementation Strategy

Since we're dealing with times and 24-hour format, I'll:
1. Convert current time to total minutes since midnight
2. Generate times in chronological order starting from current time
3. For each time, convert to string "hh:mm" and check if it's a palindrome
4. Return the difference in minutes

```python
def is_palindrome(time_str):
    # Remove colon and check if string reads the same forwards and backwards
    time_no_colon = time_str.replace(':', '')
    return time_no_colon == time_no_colon[::-1]

def time_to_minutes(hh, mm):
    return hh * 60 + mm

def minutes_to_time(total_minutes):
    hours = (total_minutes // 60) % 24
    minutes = total_minutes % 60
    return hours, minutes

def solve():
    time_str = input().strip()
    hh, mm = map(int, time_str.split(':'))
    
    # Convert current time to minutes since midnight
    current_minutes = time_to_minutes(hh, mm)
    
    # Check if current time is already palindromic
    current_time_str = f"{hh:02d}:{mm:02d}"
    if is_palindrome(current_time_str):
        return 0
    
    # Try each subsequent minute until we find a palindromic time
    total_minutes = current_minutes
    while True:
        total_minutes += 1
        if total_minutes >= 24 * 60:  # 24 hours in minutes
            total_minutes = 0  # wrap around to 00:00
            
        hours, minutes = minutes_to_time(total_minutes)
        time_str = f"{hours:02d}:{minutes:02d}"
        
        if is_palindrome(time_str):
            return total_minutes - current_minutes

print(solve())
```

The time after current time might wrap around to next day. But since the problem says "when she wakes up", and we're looking for the minimum time, we need to consider that we might go from 23:59 to 00:00.

```python
        time_str = f"{hours:02d}:{minutes:02d}"

            print(minutes_diff)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a current time in 24-hour format (hh:mm)
2. We need to find the minimum number of minutes to sleep until the next palindromic time
3. A palindromic time reads the same forwards and backwards (e.g., 05:50 → 05:50 reversed is 05:50)
4. We need to consider that times wrap around (e.g., 23:59 → 00:00)

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Understand palindrome checking
For a time "hh:mm", when reversed, it becomes "mm:hh" (but we need to check if the string reads the same).

For "05:50" → reversed is "05:50" → same → palindrome
For "05:39" → reversed is "93:50" → different → not palindrome

So we need to check if the 6-character string (hhmm) reads the same forwards and backwards.

### Step 2: Algorithm approach
1. Start from current time
2. Check if current time is a palindrome
3. If yes, return 0
4. If no, check the next time (increment minutes)
5. Continue until we find a palindromic time
6. Return the number of minutes needed

### Step 3: Time handling
- We need to handle the 24-hour cycle
- When minutes reach 60, we go to next hour
- When hours reach 24, we go to 00

### Step 4: Implementation

```python
def is_palindrome(time_str):
    # Convert to string and check if it reads the same forwards and backwards
    # For hh:mm, we need to check if the string is palindrome when reversed
    # The string is 6 characters: hh:mm
    s = time_str.replace(':', '')
    return s == s[::-1]

def get_next_palindrome(current_time):
    h, m = map(int, current_time.split(':'))
    
    # Convert current time to total minutes since 00:00
    current_minutes = h * 60 + m
    
    # Check each time from current time onwards
    while True:
        # Current time in minutes
        time_in_minutes = current_minutes
        
        # Convert back to hours and minutes
        current_h = time_in_minutes // 60
        current_m = time_in_minutes % 60
        
        # Normalize hours to 0-23
        current_h = current_h % 24
        
        # Format as hh:mm
        h_str = f"{current_h:02d}"
        m_str = f"{current_m:02d}"
        time_str = f"{h_str}:{m_str}"
        
        # Check if it's a palindrome
        if is_palindrome(time_str):
            return time_in_minutes - current_minutes
        
        # Move to next minute
        current_minutes += 1
        
        # If we've gone more than 24 hours, we should loop back to 00:00
        # But since we're looking for minimum, and 24 hours is a palindrome (00:00, 11:11, etc.),
        # we only need to check until we find one
        
        # Safety check: if we've gone too far, loop back to 00:00
        # But in practice, the answer won't be very large (maximum 120 minutes likely)
        # We can just continue incrementing
        
        # The maximum time we need to check is less than 1440 minutes (24 hours)
        # But we don't need to implement a safety limit since the problem constraints are small

# Read input
current_time = input().strip()

# Solve
result = get_next_palindrome(current_time)
print(result)
```

```python
def is_palindrome(time_str):
    # Remove colon and check if palindrome

    next_m = next_minutes % 60
```
